ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e209	The case spokesman Keshvari is on the road in the traffic, I have been told, so I will say some words on behalf of him. We are pleased that all four cooperation parties are some on a review of the principles and guidelines for cooperation between the state and private actors, with the aim of reducing public activity in well-functioning private, commercial markets. The review should be ready for next year. The representative proposal from the Left, which we are now considering, forms the basis for this decision and helps to illustrate an important and under-communicated issue. One of the reasons for the growth we see in the public sector is that the state is taking on a large scale tasks where there is a well-functioning market and strong, established professional environment outside the state. Manipulation of tasks is done by new applications and projects and partly by insourcing tasks where the market has previously solved the task. Each of these tasks may seem reliable, but the sum, in the view of the majority of the Committee, is an increasing problem. I am pleased that we have a government, supported by the Christian People's Party and the Left, which in this period has largely focused on simplification, efficiency and red tape. Simplification of private business bureaucracy will continue. Today's decision will be an important contribution in that context. Private business must focus as much as possible on productivity and, as much as possible, on authority reporting. It is important that the services provided to the inhabitants maintain a high quality, just as they operate the services. Competition in the market is better able to promote quality, innovation and efficient operation than public monopolies. Such monopolies should be able to be established underpinned by specific social conditions. In order to avoid undesirable monopolistic industries, it is therefore necessary to have a level playing field for public and private operators. In the current ageing period, neutral VAT has been introduced in the country, it has been published as a guide for increased preferences in the municipal sector and established a national strategy for the use of cloud services, and the public procurement legislation is considerably simplified. The majority in the Committee will press for new reforms in the same direction. Unfortunately, there are now several places where the development is countered locally by other pluralities of local municipal government and city government. For example, in the city we are now, the capital of the country, the majority have decided to go in the opposite direction by letting those who run private hospitals lose contracts. The same is true in the nursery sector. The hold behind this — both hold and rhetoric — is negative for the development of the country. In addition, one of the Committee's positions will see that the Committee shares the traditional right-left axis, where the majority – the Party of Government and the Christian People's Party and the Left – are united. The others might want to do something for themselves.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e228	The public sector is the pillar of the Norwegian model. The public sector and the pillar it is make Norway one of the world’s best countries to live in. We in the Workers' Party believe that public tasks financed by taxes and charges or user payments should, as a general rule, be carried out by the public. This ensures democratic governance and development of key societal areas. We believe that there is little evidence to suggest to the representatives that in the absence of competition, the State – the public – does not have the capacity to drive innovation in its areas of activity. On the contrary, by its size, knowledge base and purchasing power, the State is an initiator of innovation in both new and existing industries and an active renewer of community services. Many major public acquisitions are linked to the development and operation of IT systems. In the future, if public authorities are to have real control over their own systems, it is essential not to link unilaterally to larger suppliers and to assess which systems should operate under their own control. We also point out that, in some areas, the country competes with the private sector to attract skilled labour, especially in the field of IT. It is therefore essential that the conditions of employment in the State sector are sufficiently good to enable the State to maintain a stable workforce. We also believe that it is up to political authorities to determine which areas of society should be subject to public governance and authority. The practical implementation of the proposal will result in a significant additional cost to consultants, as well as a significant loss of operational efficiency in the commissioning of assignments and subsequent assessments. We believe that the proposal bears the mark of wanting to privatize the public sector on the basis of an ideological conviction. We also show in the notes that the country's involvement in business has been historically justified in several different respects. Economic, sectoral, regional and employment policies have been important factors. In addition, distributional considerations have often been decisive. These are considerations which, in different ways and in different areas, will remain in force in the future. I think this is the last time I'm standing on this pulpit. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e256	Are there any people who doubt that the restaurant offer in our country would be less exciting if all the restaurants had been owned by the state and run by government staff, or who do not want to maintain a free choice of competing dentists and fast doctors? No matter who finances the purchase of products and services, there are important arguments for limiting public activity in well-functioning commercial markets, not least in societies with a large and growing public sector, such as Scandinavia. Let me mention some points: Quality: Services improve when suppliers have to meet customers on a daily basis in a market and they know that lack of quality will result in job losses and reduced profits. Innovation: There is little reason for entrepreneurs and social entrepreneurs to spend time and money on innovation if they know that they are stopped by public decisions not to use commercial suppliers anyway. Oslo under red-green rule can be an example of how the creativity of the population is not fully exploited. Control: no one is good inspectors by itself. In public procurement, it is scarcely disputeable that it is easier to get rid of private suppliers than with private organisations that do not deliver well enough. Economy: All monopolies will deliver sooner or later more expensive and worse than in a competitive situation. This will weaken Norway’s competitiveness over time and thus the ability to further develop our welfare services. Public authorities should strengthen competition through good framework conditions, not weaken it by themselves becoming suppliers in areas where this is not necessary. That Works believes Municipality-Norway can save 4 billion. kr to allow private to provide more home services, and that a study by Agenda Kaupang suggests that the public has saved more than 2 billion. kr per year using private kindergartens are interesting indications. In addition, there is reason to lift a warning finger in the development of democracy in a society where a large majority depend on monthly payments from the state. Today, well over half of the country’s population depends entirely or partly on monthly payments from the public sector. Less activity in functioning markets will also give the public better time to operate its own core business. Let me make it clear that these are system arguments. There is no reason to believe or claim that there is a difference in quality or effort, whether you work in the private sector or in the public sector. Does not the public have a role in influencing the functioning of commercial markets? Yes, absolutely — through framework conditions, through procurement, and not least through the financing of very important welfare services. This is a question where the non-socialist parties think fundamentally differently than the socialists. That's why we don't need a crystal ball to know that the proposal adopted today by the four non-socialist parties will be put in the reverse drawer by a government change. It doesn't deserve Norway.

Nevertheless, we are in favour of the proposal, because we see that a debate and a practical and ideological rise in the border between what is to be solved by the state and what is to be resolved by the private is important. In the balance between system thinking, ideology and the free market, we believe that there must be a solution between them. For the Christian People's Party, however, it is very important to stress – and here we have a clear principle – that private, ideal service providers should not get in trouble between the commercial and the public. We share the opinion of the Council of State that it may be useful to wait for the working group already in the process of looking at this before we reduce a heavy and resource-intensive public sample. That is why we are pleased with the rewording made by the majority of the Committee on the original proposal from the Left, and we support the Committee's position.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e295	The representatives of the Left want to reduce public activity in what they call ‘good-functioning commercial markets’. On the contrary, we believe that the school, health and care services financed by taxes and taxes should, as a general rule, be run by the state and the municipalities – preferably supplemented by non-profit actors. In my view, the rapporteurs and the majority of the Committees have an excessively dogmatic and ideological approach to this by their comments. Clearly, reducing public activity is an objective in itself. It states that one has a fundamental belief that competition and market organisation provide better services. I can't see that the experience we have with competitive outsourcing provides such an answer. On the contrary, it often gets worse and more expensive. One of the problems with the outsourcing of competition is that it entails additional costs. It costs to build up the competence to tender and to check that the supplies given are in conformity with the contracts concluded. Privatisation of publicly funded tasks also means that the public must pay for the profits that suppliers derive from their activities. The benefits of privatisation are not so easy to find in the accounts, either the economics or the quality and user satisfaction. We have a way to go before we become good enough as sponsors to make demands that address environmentally friendly solutions and take into account the professional development of labour through the use of apprentices. One of the really big construction projects in the next decade is the government quarter. Here we have proposed that the tender criteria and contracts should be designed so that Norwegian businesses can participate in a competitive manner. It is important that we develop the public sector, both state and local authorities, to become more competent providers and orderers of services. We must make demands on efficiency, quality and environmental considerations. At that time, resources should be used other than to set up another public body to reduce public activity. We don't need that sample.

At the same time, more revenues for the same product provide more opportunities for innovation and further development, which benefit both new and old customers. In its reply letter dated 28 April, the Council of State states that the establishment and operation of reception are subject to competition and that cost-effectiveness and flexibility are necessary in a system where capacity needs can fluctuate considerably in a short time. While it is, of course, necessary to take into account the costs of both establishment and closure of reception, it is also important that quality is a central criterion of scale-down in order to ensure that the best quality receptions are maintained. A majority of the committee believes that it is unfortunate when well-run receptions that have been operational for many years are dropped because only price has been decisive. Often this reception has gained valuable experience, knowledge and competence and has a good cooperation with the community. Ideal actors bring with them added values that are important from a social point of view. Good social economy is not only about savings in the short term but also about the management of Community resources in a responsible manner. In this way, it is in the interests of society to ensure that residents receive a decent and meaningful life, so that they are well integrated into society. In the long term, this will be cost-effective. A majority of the Committee, all except for the Government Parties, sees the need to clarify the criteria to be taken into account when the reception capacity is built up or down. I am pleased that the Storting today decides to develop clear criteria for the establishment, continuation and closure of reception, which ensure a better balance between commercial, municipal and non-profit operators.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e1009	This is where the Council of State is wrong. I would like to ask the Council and the Government to read this position and the annual report from the National Institute for Human Rights. It is not a question of having a different offer for young people over and under 15 years of age, for example, but it is not possible to have a different offer for Norwegian youth over and under 15 years of age, compared with the offer given to lone minor asylum seekers over and under 15 years of age. That is what the Convention on the Rights of the Child obliges us – that we should not discriminate against children, no matter what reason they have to be in Norway. It is purely a responsibility to ensure that this is the case, regardless of whether we believe that they will be allowed to stay afterwards, or whether they will not get it, for what reason they are here, or whether we believe that they have parents who have behaved foolishly. No, that's not what the Children's Convention is about. The Convention on the Rights of the Child is about the obligation of the authorities to treat children equally and with good quality — and, of course, with due care, it should be lacking, but that is not what this is all about. It is a matter of accepting discrimination. I was present when Fafo presented his report on trafficking in human beings and refugee children. Then the Minister for Children, Solvency Horne, stood on the speaker's chair and said that all children in Norway are our children and that they should be treated with an equivalent offer. It doesn't matter to the government, and it also makes it clear to the national institution for human rights. It is the institution of the Great Britain, which we have set up precisely because there is always a drag on how to interpret these conventions, and they are clarifyingly clear: the state violates its human rights obligations towards these children, and briefly they say: the Children's Convention does not open up such discrimination between children's groups, not on the basis of immigration-regulating grounds, not on the basis of the resource situation. There are no valid immigration policy considerations that can justify such a device, they say in their submissions. That is why I would strongly urge all parties in the Chamber to look at proposal No. 5, which I have put on the table today, because there we say it should comply with the Convention on the Rights of the Child, as the National Institute for Human Rights imposes on us, and provide for an equivalent offer compared to other children who are subject to the care of the State – because that is our obligations.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e1069	The proposers ask the government to submit a proposal for a Norwegian model for a water technology development programme as a split layer between the state, the municipalities and the supplier industry. They point out that the water industry is facing major challenges in delivering satisfactory water and sewerage services in the future. According to the report Investment needs in the water and sewerage sector, released by Norwegian Water in 2013, an investment need has been estimated up to 2030 at 490 billion. kr, and the proposers point out that the development of innovative and cost-effective solutions can provide significant savings in both investment and operation, thus reducing the long-term fee for the inhabitants. The proposers also believe that the State must play a more active role in this important area of society and contribute to the establishment of research and technology development programmes in the water and sewerage sectors. Local authorities mainly finance the necessary investments in the water and sewerage sector through the charges paid by subscribers and do not charge the State budget. The large investments will lead to more expensive water and sewerage services in the future for residents and businesses, and a joint committee believes that it should be committed to developing more cost-effective and resilient technological solutions in the water and sewerage sectors in order to obtain the best possible and cheapest possible services in the future. A joint committee also refers to the municipal proposition for 2018, where the government mentions a new drinking water regulation requiring consideration of drinking water in municipal plans, and indicates that a new drinking water regulation has been adopted, which was adopted in force on 1 January 2017. It makes explicit demands on municipalities to take care of drinking water when drawing up the area part of the municipal plan and when granting permits under the relevant legislation. The Committee also points out that, if necessary, municipalities should take the initiative in inter-municipal cooperation in order to deal with drinking water where the water supply system is located in several municipalities. Municipalities shall also cooperate with water owners and assess the need for restrictions to protect the raw water sources and the water catchment area. The county council shall take account of drinking water in drawing up regional plans accordingly. According to the Water and Sewage Plant Act, the municipalities are mainly owned by the water and sewerage plants in Norway. Rehabilitation and a possible technology development programme are therefore the responsibility of municipalities. It is the municipalities, as owners of the water and sewerage systems, that organise and, where appropriate, cooperate in order to achieve the necessary technological development. That is why we do not support this proposal either.

That's what we think is going to be interfering with municipal autonomy. So in this setting, there's two considerations to take. One is for the small airline environment in the eastern part of the world, which we ask the government to make sure that there is a place to be after Kjeller's closure, and the other is for the urban development in Skedsmo.

The lack of transparency is serious, but it is the great seriousness of the Riksrevision report that is, after all, the most important. As shown in the setting, it is split. In particular, the Right Party and the Advance Party disagree with the majority’s view on the issue of secrecy, and they disagree with the conclusions of the National Audit Office. The left largely agrees with the analyses, but reserves itself against the concept of ‘strongly critical’, which I will come back to later in the article. The starting point for the Riksrevision study is 22 July 2011, when Norway was hit by the most serious terrorist incident ever in peacetime. The nation was united in grief, but also in despair that society had not been better able to stop the perpetrator. A special committee, chaired by Knut Arild Hareide, was set up. It gave its position on 1 March 2012. Alexandra Bech Gjørv was assigned to lead a commission, and it was clearly communicated that the Commission would write the July 22 story undefiled and honest. The six main conclusions of the Commission were crushing. In its statement of 28 August 2012, the Prime Minister gave the following recognition to the Stortinget: ‘The Commission demonstrates a failure in Norwegian preparedness and preparedness culture that is more extensive and deeper than I was prepared for. It is difficult to take over." Prime Minister Stoltenberg lamented the failure of preparedness. An overall political Norway agreed that the recommendations of the Gjørv Commission, as proposed by the Special Committee, should be followed up. Important measures were already under work or under way by the then government. An important part of the follow-up was precisely the instruction, entitled ‘Instructions on the Protection and Protection of Objects in the Use of Defence and Police Security Forces in Peace, Crisis and War’, which was established at kgl. resolution of 24 August 2012, with effect from the same day. It is the compliance of the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Justice and Defence with this directive that the Riksrevision has examined, and that is what this matter is about today. In addition, it has been examined whether the Defence and Police Directorate are complying with the Security Act and with the Object Protection and Basic Protection Regulation of their own security-worthy features. The Committee notes that the Riksrevision made the following main findings in its studies: Firstly, the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the Ministry of Defence have not strengthened and improved cooperation between the police and the defence. Important measures to ensure good cooperation and preparation have not been implemented. Thus, there is a risk that important objects will not be able to maintain their operations or functionality in critical situations. The two departments have also failed to ensure that the Directorate of Police and Defence have established a permanent basic security for their own protective features. The final remark of the National Audit Office is that neither the Minister of Justice nor the Minister of Defence has given a full explanation as to why the work on object security and basic security has not been given the necessary priority. The Directorate of Police and Defence, the former and the current Minister of Justice and Preparedness, the Minister of Defence and the Prime Minister have agreed to the Riksrevision's findings and criticism. However, the Chief of Defense, as I see it affordable, is present here today, said in the open hearing that he disagreed with the Riksrevision that there is a likelihood that neither the police nor the Defense, together or individually, will be able to exercise adequate protection of important objects when a threat situation warrants it. In reply to Mr Gruvean's question about the Minister for Defence, the Minister for Defence agreed with the Riksrevision's conclusion, the Minister for Defence said: «As I was in the audience, I agree with the findings and recommendations and the main conclusions that come, but I also needed to say that we have the ability to secure.» The majority believe that the conclusions of the National Audit Office are correct. The finds of the Riksrevision have done, substantiating this. However, the members of the Committee from the Right and the Advance Party refer to what the head of defence said at the open hearing that he did not share the conclusion of the Riksrevision. I would like to say it this way: it is a very remarkable situation we are facing here – that the members of the Right Party and the Progressive Party, the representatives of the government parties, choose to agree with the head of defence rather than their own State Council. At the open hearing, the Prime Minister said: ‘The government was not aware that the situation was like this until we actually received the Riksaudition report and got a status on it. That was when you first saw that it was worse. So it is that both the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Justice are among the areas that under this government have been economically priorityed. Home protection has been a priority in economic terms, giving it greater opportunities, the defence in general has been a priority in economic terms, and the Ministry of Justice has received considerably increased appropriations every year.’ This is probably some of the answer to why the Prime Minister's office was not aware of the situation before the Riksrevision's report became known. The assumption has been that emergency work has been on track. At the same time, the Gjørv Commission identifies this as a specific challenge for any government: the follow-up to the achievement of results, rather than the assumption that they are, is crucial. Alexandra Bech Gjørv said it this way: ‘Following the fact that the result is actually achieved is a very important lesson that we can all learn, and all of you who are in responsible positions must make sure that there is a change in it. That's what we're asking for." The Prime Minister said in the Questionnaire on 31 May 2017: "I also said that we were too bad. I think we should admit that. There was too poor follow-up of the object protection.’;» This is not a tear-off quote. A central part of the preparedness as object protection is was not followed up by the responsible state councils. It is characterised by the Riksrevisionen as ‘very serious’. That term is used exceptionally, e.g. where there is a risk to life and health. The majority of the Committee agrees with this and considers that the lack of priority of object protection is therefore highly critical. The majority believe that neglect of such a character should have been caught by a coordinating body at the Prime Minister's Office, whose primary task is to ensure stronger and faster governance of social security and national preparedness. It obviously hasn't happened. That too is regrettable. The National Audit Office has also examined whether the Defence and Police Directorate comply with the requirements for basic protection of their own protective features. The report shows that the two departments have failed to do so, despite the fact that the Storting clearly stressed that measures of this kind should be implemented without delay. Alexandra Bech Gjørv said about the risk awareness challenge, that you intellectually know that it can happen, but that you really don't think it happens. But the government of Stoltenberg took responsibility and started an extensive work with a view to improving preparedness. The majority promised the people and each other that we would not even stand unprepared, and gathered behind a series of measures to correct all the errors that had been revealed. Representatives of the right parties used the lack of security on the black day of July 2011 to promote their own implementation power in 2013. I even remember that representatives of the right-wing parties from this pulpit suggested that this would not happen if they had run. When we know all this and have it as a back cover for the handling of this matter, I would say that then there is no excuse for not having done what the Stortinge decided to do, and that we all agreed – not now and not after 22 July 2011. However, the majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the members of the Right and the Progressive Party, is of the opinion that the learning that would result from the review of the fatal incident in 2011 is most important. SEK for basic protection in the Defence for the period 2012-2016 and an additional SEK 450 million. kr is set aside in the long-term plan. Also in the police, significant investments have been made in object protection, such as: ICT infrastructure supporting critical command and control functions has been moved to safer locations. These are operations that necessarily take time. Through the Gjørv report after the 22nd of July, a number of measures were recommended that should be implemented. In December 2016, Meld was notified. St. 10 for 2015–2016 Risk in a safe society presented. An overview of the status of the measures recommended following the terrorist acts is provided in Annex 2 to the notification. The list contains a total of 116 different measures. 102 of these have been accepted as carried out, while 14 measures have the status of being at work. A number of the latter category have to be considered today. This is a very good evidence that both the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the Ministry of Defence have worked continuously to complete all the tasks pointed out by the Gjørv Commission report. We believe it would be to cross a line if the Storting published classified information against the government's will. The size could override the safety code by means of a special law. Such a special law would be completely contrary to the principle of the Security Act, namely that the issuer of the information is the one to decide on grading. The Christian People's Party believes that we should not give ourselves a legal home that actually makes the Storting a case handler for individual documents that the security authorities are grading. If the Storting takes ownership of secret government documents, the result can actually be less transparency, and no more. Exercising power must have confidence that the Storting process the information correctly. Only then can the Storting exercise its important control function. At the same time, there must be equal practices regardless of whether there is minority government or majority government. Respect for the Security Act must be equal regardless of whether a government has a majority or a minority in the Storting. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e1933	I would like to thank the Committee for its thorough work on an attitude based on the Riksrevision's classified report, which no one has proposed to downgrade. I look seriously at the weaknesses pointed out by the National Audit Office in the field of object protection, and the main findings of the National Audit Office have been and will be very important in the further improvement work. But I would also like to stress that the continuous work on object protection gives a different status today than when the Riksaudition conducted its studies for the financial year 2015. A lot of work has been going on for a long time, even before the Riksrevision made its report, and we are still not in the finish line. I think it is necessary in that context to correct something that is in the vote by the majority. On page 11, it says: ‘One thing is that the errors have been corrected, as claimed by the Minister of Defence at the open hearing and in the Stortinget. [[...]] I have not at any time – and this is not clear from the minutes – said that all errors should be corrected. On the contrary, I have said that a lot of work remains, although a lot of work is in place. The defence is criticised in the audit for not identifying the necessary civil key objects. When the defence began updating the old list from November 2005 with civil and military key objects, it was relevant to see this in conjunction with the ongoing revitalisation of national operational planning. Therefore, the military objects were first designated. The designation of the civil objects took place at the same time as the National Audit Office's investigation. In the list from December 2016, the civil objects had increased in number. There was an expected increase, and the defense had close cooperation with civil authorities in this process. Thus, no single key item was identified between November 2005 and 2012. The 2012 instruction led to a change in relation to the old Key Point Directive by the fact that the key objects can now be only objects of crucial importance for defence and military defence in war, and thus be considered legal objectives in war. The old key objects were objects of significance both for military defence and for civil protection in total defence. Throughout this period, the government has emphasised improvement measures for Home Affairs, in particular in the field of materials and logistics. The main priority of the new long-term plan is to strengthen the operational capacity of Defence, and to a large extent for Home Affairs. I can therefore not recognise myself in, or stand behind, the representative Raja's statement that HV is a rib. On the contrary, the Heimevertet will be strengthened for 2017 alone by 191 million. kr, or 16 pst. budget increase. It is set by money for object protection material. Since 2015, we have been driving a comprehensive rearming of the Heimewertel to new and modern weapons. We're now acquiring fieldcars for over 0.5 billion. kr, and we are doing great exercises this year to maintain emergency equipment in the Home and Army. It's very important, and it continues the work we started already when we joined. Thus, the lack of adequate operational capability in all areas does not mean that the Defence and Home Protection are incapacitated for object protection. This is not a either-or-relationship. The defense has a central key object list which is now kept up to date. At the beginning of December 2016 there were object plans for approx. 96 pst. by the objects. When a new list was adopted in December, the number of objects, as I said, was increased, and in April it had 87 pst. object plane. This is a living document, so periodically there will be objects without a completed object plane, but they can still be secured. In 2017, the Heimevertet has planned to exercise on a large proportion of both civil and military objects. This natural dynamics are absolutely necessary and important in order to have the best possible protection of what is at all times the most important objects, and we will never be finished with object protection – precisely because new objects and other threat images are going to emulate the work of planning and securing. The government has generally prioritised strengthening cooperation between the police and the defence. There is now a good opportunity for better and more effective cooperation today than it did in the past. In 2017, good cooperation is reported from both the Armed Forces Operational Headquarters’ cooperation with the Police Directorate and the Home Guard Districts’ cooperation with the police districts. Object backup is a theme in meetings and cooperation forums. Fixed weekly updates via videoconferencing and exchange ofliaisons contribute to good cooperation climate and low threshold for dialogue. With regard to plans for those objects where the police are requesting pre-planned assistance from the Defense Department, now 72 pst. with plans – against 25 pst. when the Riksrevision conducted its investigation. The presentation of the Royal Resolution of 24 August 2012 on instructions for the use of securing forces clearly presupposes a realistic assessment of the resources available for different types of situations and, in particular, a rapid re-prioritisation of the forces. So it's going to be situational which objects are to be secured, depending on the type of threat, the spatial threat picture, the type of objects and the importance of the objects. When the mechanisms in the instructions are used, it is considered that, in most cases, the current Home Security Forces, together with the police forces, will be sufficient for police protection. There is also a planned exercise from the Home Office at approx. one third of the pre-designated objects in 2017. In the case of permanent basic protection, it is important to be aware that all defence shielding features have a form of permanent basic protection. About 55 pst. they are in military camps and are then normally subject to day-to-day access control and watchkeeping. The defense has more than 60 pst. of all displayable objects, and it is important to understand that this too is a dynamic object list. The state of basic protection is better today than in 2015, and risk assessments have been carried out for all displayable objects today. In 2015 it was not. We also know that part of the shielding objects are in camps that – either on the basis of the previous or this long-term plan – are to be phased out. In recent years, large sums have been spent on basic security in the Defence. For the five-year period 2012-2016, hedging measures for SEK 1.1 billion have been implemented. kr only in existing building mass. For the last two years, 2015–2016, the sum is 432 million. kr. In addition to that, the next few years are set at 450 million. kr for securing in existing buildings, plus that all new buildings have basic security as part of the design. It is also important to stress that work is in progress for approx. 930 million. kr to replace radio lines with new fibre capacity and it reduces the number of shielding objects and greatly increases the resilience. The open hearing drew attention to the oil and gas sector. I'm not going to go into details here, because I've done that in the closed forums, but I just want to stress that the police and the defense can secure facilities in this sector with security forces, even if they're not designated as protective objects. Both floors are prepared and have mapped fuses. It's important to underline. This review shows that the important findings of the Riksrevision have been addressed. Many of the findings have already been followed up. More people are at work. Some require long-term follow-up, and the work is well under way. In addition, the government has worked specifically with two aspects at the same time. One is working on new assistance instructions for defence assistance to the police. There are now simpler and shorter guidelines and not least a clearer division of responsibilities. In addition, the government has also presented a new safety law. The update of a law of almost twenty years, which is not adapted to today's threat picture, was absolutely necessary. We did it in two stages to make sure that we did the most important thing first, and I look forward to the Storting being able to process the Safety Act during the year.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e2065	The legitimacy of the State rests on its ability to take care of the security of its citizens. At the same time, it is important to stress that this was the situation in 2015. In the closed hearing on 29 May this year and during yesterday's treatment of the classified setting, I explained the status today, both in terms of instructions and compliance with the Safety Act and the associated regulations on object safety. The work on meeting the requirements of the instructions has now come a long way, and the Directorate of Police has developed a system for the continuous follow-up and maintenance of the plan of objects in the future, as well as related exercises. In my opinion, this is very satisfactory. In addition, it is important for me to say that today we are quite different from the 22nd of July events in the area of security and preparedness. Since the government joined, there have been nearly 1,800 more annual police work. This means a significant strengthening of basic preparedness and is due to a historically high police force at the end of 2017. I would also like to stress that under this government, demands have been made for police response time for missions that require immediate call-out. The response time requirement was introduced on 1 January 2015 and measurements show that the police meet the required requirements. New instructions for the police came into force on 1 April 2016. This instruction contributes to a shorter response time and better preparedness. On 16 June this year, the Government issued new instructions for the defence assistance to the police. The instruction will ensure that decisions on assistance from the Defence to the police can be taken as quickly as possible in a crisis situation. In the revised National Budget 2017, the Government proposes to invest in three new, more-capable police helicopters and aims at delivery during 2019. New rescue helicopters to replace the current Sea King fleet shall, as is known, be delivered between 2017 and 2020. Means have been allocated for the planning and design of a National Police Emergency Response Centre. The response rate has been significantly strengthened and the PST has been significantly strengthened by nearly 300 million. kr during this period of government. In addition, through the revised national budget, we have also provided 100 million. kr more for cars and equipment for the police. The PST National Operation Centre opened in January 2017. The situation centre of the police opened on 1 January 2016 and the Joint Cyber Coordination Centre opened on 31 March this year. A national procedure on the interoperability of emergency situations in the event of ongoing life-threatening violence, PLIVO, was established with effect from February 2015. Even though a lot of things have improved, that doesn't mean we're done. This is a continuous work. However, the work done leaves little doubt that a significant effort has been made by the government to strengthen social security and preparedness. Cooperation between departments at all levels of crisis management is a high priority, both in the justice and emergency sectors. I have traveled around the police districts and talked to police officers at all levels, and the consistent answer is that the cooperation between police and defence is extensive and good in daily life. However, it is not that the characteristic of good cooperation is the absence of any disagreement, but I would like to stress that my opinion is that the cooperation between police and defence is very good in most areas. In terms of object safety by law and regulation, the police object was secured by what we refer to as temporary compensatory security measures in 2015. The national audit also points out that the objects are secured by temporary measures. As I read a lot of the criticism from the Riksrevision, it goes on that there were no concrete plans for permanent protection of objects. Some of this can be explained by the fact that it has not been possible or reasonable on the basis of a cost-benefit assessment to implement more permanent and robust measures where the objects are – or have been – located. After the audit, a significant work has been done on object safety, which I explained in detail during yesterday's treatment of the classified setting. I note that the wording of the 2016 budget proposal has become a topic in the vote. The text states that in December 2014, the Ministry informed the NSM that all the features were secured in accordance with the Code. In retrospect, the Department sees that the wording can give a somewhat misleading impression. The review of the area was therefore adjusted in the 2017 budget proposal.To be more concrete, the police have not very many objects in total. The objects classified are essentially functions that support command and control, critical ICT components and some national capacities that the police have. Following the audit, a major infrastructure project has been carried out to relocate one of the features, and several relocation processes are in progress, with concrete plans for permanent securing most of the features. Furthermore, the police are looking at the possibility of better redundancy on critical functions classified as protective objects. Examples include better and more flexible ICT solutions, changes in procedures or other administrative measures. Finally, it is important to mention that object safety is a broad field of expertise in which a number of measures in total contribute to increased protection of objects. For example, a reduction in police response time, as well as training, competence and equipment provided by the police, will help to improve the protection of cross-sectoral protective features. These measures will also support the securing of objects following the instructions in a similar way. And I can assure you that today's work on object protection has a high priority from me and my department.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e2597	This case concerns the National Audit Office’s investigation into the sale of two F-5 fighter aircraft by the Defence Agency and has its background in the case of the sale of used and unarranged defence equipment, cf. Inner. 213 S for 2015-2016 and Innst. 213 S Appendix 1 for 2015-2016. The case of used and discontinued defence equipment started already in 2014, when Dagbladet had a notice on the sale of marine vessels to Nigeria. During the examination of this case, the Committee was subsequently also made aware of the sale of two F-5 fighter aircraft, which were sold to a private company in the United States. This sale was also included in the hearings on the case of the sale of end-of-life marine vessels, and since the Committee was not satisfied with the responses of the fighter aircraft, during the hearing, a majority of the Stortinget Riksrevision asked to review the entire sales process of the two F-5 fighter aircraft operating in the United States and sold to the US company Northern General Leasing LCC, NGL, in 2015. As early as 1999, the majority decided to phase out 15 F-5 fighter aircraft. These have since been tried to sell to several stakeholders. A total of 15 stakeholders have been involved in attempts to sell the aircraft. After ten planes were subsequently donated to museums and educational institutions, two aircraft were sold in 2015 to Northern General Leasing, NGL. The main findings of the National Audit Office following a review of the sales processes from 2003 to 2015 have shown a number of deficiencies in the disposal process: The defence and the Ministry of Defence had a lack of understanding of the export control legislation in 2003. In the sale, the US authorities had a decisive influence on the use of F-5 aircraft. The defence's follow-up to the work on disposing of military material has not been good enough. The Ministry of Defence has failed to discharge its overall responsibility in the area of disposal properly. There has been weak traceability in the process of selling the F-5 aeroplanes. There has been a breach of the principle of equal treatment of stakeholders. The Northern General Leasing LCC received a competitive advantage over other stakeholders due to the storage of the aircraft. The follow-up of stakeholders by the defence logistics organisation has been different. The defence logistical organisation’s requirements for stakeholders have been different. The final sale did not facilitate maximum dividends. The National Audit Office’s investigation has been based on a number of decisions and assumptions from the Storting. I would like to highlight the following, although it is not exhaustive: Act on Control of Exports of Strategic Goods, Services and Technology, etc. export control laws; normal instructions for the stripping and cassation of materials and buildings and for the disposal of property belonging to the country, i.e. the normal instructions The Committee points out that weapons cannot be sold to countries or territories where there is war or where war threatens. This is a principle which was laid down as early as 1959 and laid down in the Export Control Act. On several occasions, from 2003, the defense has tried to sell F-5 fighter aircraft to land and non-licensed companies. It shows that neither the Ministry of Defence nor the Ministry of Foreign Affairs have had a good enough understanding of the export control legislation. The Committee is of the opinion that these sales attempts should have been stopped at an earlier date by the Ministry of Defence as an exporter or by the Ministry of Defence in dialogue with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Already when it became known that there were countries and/or companies that obviously would not have been licensed, the sales trials should have stopped. In 2009, a contact forum was set up to provide a better understanding of the Export Control Code in Defence. However, this ended in 2011 as a result of a letter from a department in the Ministry of Defence other than the department participating in the Contact Forum together with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the FLO. The contact forum only started again in 2015. The Progress Party and the Central Party agree with the Riksrevision that it was unfortunate that the meetings of the Contact Forum were stopped between 2011 and 2015. But since 2015, according to the Ministry of Defence, there have been regular meetings between the Ministry of Defence, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Defence and Defence, which were established in 2016. The Riksrevision believes that the follow-up to the attempts to sell the F-5 aircraft has been too weak and that neither the management of the FLO nor the Defence Staff has ensured that the laws and regulations have been complied with or that the traceability requirements have been met. The National Audit Office considers this to be critical, and the Committee agrees with the Riksrevision. Furthermore, the Committee would point out that there is no lack of formal legislation that makes the disposal defective but that it is the actual performance of the work that is too poor. The Committee would stress that the weak traceability and lack of record-keeping of the sales processes of the F-5 aeroplanes are not in line with the requirements of sound procedures and good management practice. This is critical. The Committee points out that the Council of State supports the assessment that the follow-up of the defence to the disposal of military material has not been good enough and states that in recent years the Defence and Defence Ministry have taken a number of measures to ensure sound management in the event of the disposal of military equipment. The Committee also finds it critical in this case that not all stakeholders have been treated as equivalent in the sales process. The Committee would point out that the sale of defence equipment is initially to be carried out in a way that provides the greatest economic benefit and that several of the sales trials were rejected as unfounded. However, the Committee has noted that several of the sales trials were stopped when it was found difficult to get the sales authorised by the US authorities. In order to effect the sale, the FLO finally offered the aircraft at a symbolic price to the Spanish authorities, which, however, rejected this. It has recently emerged that the NGL had overheard the price at which the Spanish authorities were offered the planes, but although it was unfortunate that this was overheard, probably the price of $30,000 was the only realistic solution at that time. The Committee understands that in 2015 the FLO finally chose to dispose of the aeroplanes at a price under previous price offers per F-5 aircraft for the period 2003-2015, but considers that such a long disposal process is too long. The Committee has noted that although the Riksrevision has revealed many defects and defects in the disposal of the F-5 aircraft, the Riksrevision has not found any evidence or evidence to suggest any defects or corruption in the sales. Furthermore, it has not been revealed that the aeroplanes have been used without approval and under satisfactory control. The Committee considers this to be reassuring. During the hearing on the sale of military equipment, questions were asked from the case rapporteur to both the head of defence and FLO regarding the sale of the aircraft were ‘green’. Both confirmed that the sale was ‘green’. The question to the Defence Manager and Head of the FLO at that hearing on whether the sale was ‘green’ was put in the light of the fact that this was a disposal case in which out-of-court military material was to be disposed of. With regard to the deficiencies identified in the Riksrevision report regarding compliance with the legislation on the sales process of the aircraft, the Committee considers that there was no basis for the opinion of the Defence Manager and Chief FLO at that hearing that the sale of the fighter aircraft was ‘green’, even if the last part of the sale to NGL is green. In the course of the sales process, the Riksrevision has revealed major shortcomings, which the Committee considers to be critical. In conclusion, the Committee points out that it remains to be expected that there will be a need to dispose of used defence equipment, but the Committee assumes that the disposal takes place in an orderly manner and in line with the existing legislation.

There would be considerable storage or destruction costs, and I also notice that the Committee understands that in 2015 the FLO finally chose to dispose of it at a price under previous price offers.

The Riksrevision presented its study on 6 June 2017, which identified significant failures in the planning and implementation of Stortinget’s construction project. The National Audit Office's investigation is summarised with the main findings of the vote – I am aware of that – but I would nevertheless like to read them here from the pulpit. The Court of Auditors study is summarised as follows: The project has had a significant increase in costs which can only be explained in part by the change in character of the project. More emphasis has been placed on timetable and functionality than on ensuring cost control. The project organisation has not been adapted to the complexity and scope of the project. Inadequate investigations are the basis for costly choices. Better quality assurance could have revealed weaknesses in the construction project. There is a lack of legislation to ensure the proper and effective implementation of large-scale construction projects. Extension of the design agreement did not comply with procurement law. The size decision to grant money until the project is taken on an incomplete basis. There is a need to clarify roles and responsibilities between the bodies in the Grand Duchy in administrative matters. The Committee notes that the main findings are very serious and confirm the Committee's position on the budget, that the very significant exceedances are due to serious shortcomings in the planning and implementation of the project, that the builder himself is responsible, and that the presidency and the administration, which constitute the function of builder, are responsible for informing the Storting and proposing the annual appropriations through the budget proposals. The National Audit Office considers that no quality assurance has been carried out in line with the Financial Management Regulations of the State. The national audit considers this to be problematic and that such quality assurance could reveal weaknesses in the project. The National Audit Office considers it problematic that the administrative management of the Stortingen did not comply with the procurement rules in the extension of the design agreement. When you start to cross the financial limits of the investment, it introduces quality requirements that Norwegian economists have even been the world's leading developer, where you always start with the zero option: what if we don't implement this measure? Is there any other way to solve this? Now you've used the reverse technique. You have adopted one more project, then one more project, and then one more project, and then you see where the journey ends. It is not commendable, and that is why we have rules on it. We've done that here, but what we need isn't really new rules. We need compliance with those we have. Of course, they can say no. What has evolved over time is that first the presidency has actually taken all the decisions and assumed possible liability if we were to break it. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e3014	I would first like to thank the rapporteur and the other members of the Committee for a large and in many ways difficult work on this matter, not least because this is the Stortinge's own construction business. Unfortunately, this case is an example of a building case which both the Grand Duchy's presidency, the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee and the taxpayers would like to be in addition. Why? Well, when a decision in the presidency in April 2011 to rehabilitate the Prince's Gate 26, i.e., P26, started as a relatively simple refurbishment project to $70 million. kr, but ended up as a major rehabilitation job with several additional functions, with a total cost of SEK 1,8 billion. kr, one has to ask himself how this could happen. As a result of the terrorist action on 22 July 2011, there was a strong focus on object protection, and one then the possibility of integrating measures to increase safety in the building as part of the refurbishment project. This was natural, when the July 22 terror probably shook everyone. The first estimate for the total refurbishment, which was then made by P26, was between 272 and 365 million. kr. As a result of the need for increased security, the Presidency decided to include the construction of new postal and goods receivers in the new project. The cost of total renovation of P26 and mail and goods receipts was now estimated to be 660 million. kr. This estimate did not include the cost of new entry into the tunnel to the garage from Wesselsplad/Nedre Vollgate. The 2014 draft budget, which was discussed in Stortinget, did not mention any costs of re-entry and tunneling to the garage from Wesselsplad/Nedre Vollgate, although the Storting administration worked with two different options for re-entry and had a preliminary calculation for this when the budget proposal was discussed. On 5 December 2013, the Presidency decided that the new entry should be moved to the City Hall Gate, and the reason for this was objections from neighbours and Oslo Municipality’s plans for collective transport over Wessel’s place. A new cost framework was estimated at 847 million. kr, where the additional cost of moving the drive-in to the town hall gate was estimated at 80 million. kr. In spring 2014, the cost framework for the entire construction project increased to 1,137 million. kr, which was promoted in the 2015 budget. In June 2015, it increased again to 1,450 million. kr, in the context of the 2016 draft budget. In September 2016, the cost estimate was adjusted once again up to 1,800 million. kr, in the 2017 budgetary procedure. In the cost estimate, like the previous estimates, an uncertainty estimate was included. During the 2017 budgetary review, the Committee was very critical of the exceedances in the development of the P26 as well as the problem of accessing accurate and up-to-date information on the project. The Committee agreed with the Stortinget that the Stortinget should ask the National Audit Board to review the entire building project Prince's Gate 26 mv. The Riksrevision presented its studies on 6 June 2017 and the Committee found that the Riksrevision found significant failure in the planning and implementation of the Stortingen construction project. The main conclusion of the Court of Auditors is set out in nine points. I'm not going to repeat these nine points now, but maybe the first four, which at least I think are important: «The construction project has had a substantial cost increase which can only be explained in part by the change in character of the project. More emphasis has been placed on timetable and functionality than on ensuring cost control. The project organisation has not been adapted to the complexity and scope of the project. Inadequate investigations are at the root of costly choices.’ This is just four out of nine points. The findings of the Riksrevision in this investigation are very serious, and the Progress Party believes that the Presidency of the Storting has acted very critically in this matter. The findings confirm in its budget statement that the significant exceedances are due to serious shortcomings in the planning and implementation of the project. The Progress Party is critical that State Building, which has the necessary competence, was not involved in the project before the autumn of 2013. The Presidency and the Administration of the Storting should have realized that they did not have sufficient competence to carry out such a large and complicated project alongside their daily tasks. One of the reasons for the excess costs is that others have made representations to the project without having critical questions about their necessity and the costs they would entail. This applies, for example: the façade that the Riksantikvaren wished to preserve, and the need for an additional entry into the garage and the postal receiver, as requested by the security officers. In this case, it is not demonstrated that the performance of the façade is a claim of the Antiquarian authorities. Furthermore, there is evidence from the safety authorities on re-run requirements. It is not unusual to want to be promoted, but it is the builder who has to decide on the need and costs involved. The Presidency is responsible for keeping the Storting informed and providing accurate and complementary information on the construction project through the annual appropriations. In examining the 2014 State budget, the updated estimate for the construction project, which was not submitted to Stortinget before the adoption of the decision, was available. The consequences were, among other things, that the decision of the Storting to grant money to the project has been taken on an incomplete basis. Constitutionally, this is very objectionable. In addition, the new estimates showed that the amount limits for the implementation of external quality assurance were exceeded. If the Storting had knowledge of this, Stortinget could have returned the matter to have KS1, i.e. quality assurance. The Progress Party also considers that it is critical that the Storting did not comply with the procurement rules in the extension of the design agreement. The Party of Progress will clarify that responsibility for the exceedances, etc. seventh and last, the Stortinget, of the Presidentship as a builder, regardless of whether others, too, can be reproached for shortcomings in the planning and implementation of the project. The Progress Party therefore fully supports the conclusions of the National Audit Office, and I would quote them from: ‘The Court of Auditors considers it problematic that the Presidency of the Grand Duchy did not ensure sufficient capacity and competence for the planning and implementation of such a large and complex construction project. The National Audit Office considers that no quality assurance has been carried out in line with the Financial Management Regulations of the State. The national audit considers this to be problematic and that such quality assurance could reveal weaknesses in the project. The National Audit Office also considers it problematic that Storting’s administrative management did not comply with the procurement rules when extending the design agreement. The Court of Auditors considers that it is problematic that the plenary session of the 2014 budgetary procedure did not receive information that the cost of the project would increase, all the time when the increase in costs was known in the administrative management of the Stortinge at least one week before the deleveraging proceedings. [[...]] The party of progress joins these comments of the Riksrevision. Furthermore, the Progress Party refers to the Committee's recommendation to the Storting and, for its part, would highlight in particular the proposals for Decisions I and II. In the proposal for you, we say: ‘The Grand Duchy requests the Presidency to follow up the recommendations of the National Audit Office and to carry out a thorough review of the roles and responsibilities of the bodies of the Grand Duchy in administrative matters.’ In the proposal for II, we say: ‘The size of the Presidency is requested to ensure that an evaluation report is made after the construction project with learning points The size of the building can be taken into account in subsequent construction projects.’ The progress party considers the last point to be particularly important, given that the size of the garage in its time had similar exceedances, without we can see that there is any evaluation report as a learning base from then on. As late as today, the Committee received a copy of a letter to the parliamentary leaders from the President informing it that the 1.8-bn framework of the project. kr seems to keep going. Furthermore, the President announces that the criticism that has been made is very serious and that work has been started on a review, with a view to the necessary changes, in particular with regard to changes in plans and legislation for the Storting building project. The Progress Party considers the President’s communication to the Committee – although it is a copy – to be very positive. The Progress Party does not agree with the minority's proposal of distrust. Furthermore, the Party of Progress fully adheres to the Committee's recommendations.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e3089	I would first like to thank the case rapporteur for the job that has been done, in a very demanding case. The National Audit Office's survey of the construction project in Prince's Gate 26, new mail and goods reception and entry tunnel, has been carried out in a thorough manner, in our opinion, on the basis of the assumptions made. On the basis of the mandate given, the National Audit Board was to review and assess the economic development and progress of the construction project, further find answers to the reasons for changes in the cost, schedule and scope of the project and finally assess whether the Storting was provided with timely and comprehensive information on the construction project. However, it is also important to remember what the Riksrevision has not assessed, among other things. the role and responsibilities of external advisers and the legal contracts concluded by the administrative management of the Storting. Nor was the fulfilment by the parties of the requirements of the contract, the eligibility of any claims or the distribution of responsibilities in respect of excess costs assessed. However, what is absolutely essential for the Storting is that none of the facts that the Riksrevision has not assessed, or has failed to address, could have altered the findings and conclusions of the Riksrevision based on its investigation. Before I go into any of the conclusions, however, there is a need to stress that there was a great deal of consensus that planning and implementing projects aimed at increasing security after 22 July 2011 and trying to find solutions for a need for land expansion were important purposes, supported by the Christian People's Party. The national audit has concluded with four areas that one finds problematic. The lack of sufficient capacity and competence for the planning and implementation of such a large project is important to learn from. Based on the National Audit Office's studies and assessments, State Building could have contributed to increased competence and helped to alleviate the lack of competence that was then felt to be a professional builder. It is also interesting to note that the Presidency shares the Riksrevision’s view that the Storting should consider a solution for the future in which State Building can serve as support for the Stortinget building for future projects. Today, as has already been mentioned, we have received a copy of a letter from the Presidency to the parliamentary leaders, which shows that the criticism of the Riksrevision has been taken seriously by setting up a working group to assess the necessary changes in legislation and practice in the planning and implementation of the Stortinges building projects, based on the Riksrevision's points. We in the Christian People's Party think that's good. The National Audit Office's report states that the first contact with State Building took place in the autumn of 2013. This means that the question of professional assistance of the type represented by State Building was not raised until the planning of the project was well under way and important decisions had already been taken. Although there could be formal things, as we understand it had to be clarified, of a more constitutional nature so that state-building could take on such a role towards the Stortinget, the studies presented by the Riksrevision and of what is stated by the Presidency show that these clarifications were never properly carried out. On the contrary, the obstacles, including the alleged capacity constraints that were claimed to exist in State Building, do not seem to be real. The fact that this important issue was not clarified was clearly unfortunate in view of the further development of the project. Secondly, the Riksrevision points out that no quality assurance has been carried out in line with the Financial Management Regulations of the State, a quality assurance that could have revealed – early – weaknesses in the project. This is also a matter which we record as having been supported by the presidency. Then it's right to remind each other that it was the previous presidency, not today, who was responsible for this breach of the procurement rules. External quality assurance of cost estimates for all State projects in excess of 750 million kr shall be carried out before the project is promoted to the Stortinget. There is reason to believe that this type of action could have revealed that the project needed further processing before an elected representative went ahead with the case and presented it to the Stortinget. The serious thing is that the Storting thus made decisions to grant money on an incomplete basis. Then we record that the National Audit Office points out that there have been little assessments of the costs of different security measures. It may seem that a lack of real needs assessment of the measures has been carried out, or we should call it reassessment, as the project increased in complexity. Instead of challenging the advice given to one from the Municipality of Oslo and from neighbours, to mention some of the advisers, it may seem that one has accepted and more closely adapted to the advice, and too little has discussed and analysed the relationship between the costs and benefits of the different options. In other words, it seems that avoiding conflicts with those who gave advice has gone beyond cost control. The National Audit Office’s report shows that attention has been paid to the possibilities for extending the project, while maintaining the original migration schedule after the decision to roll over in 2017. This focus on progress, which in itself is positive, also seems to have played a negative role here because the administration has not taken sufficient time to make necessary and thorough assessments, and perhaps not least investigations, after the project has been greatly scaled up. Yes, the Riksrevision actually writes it this way in its report: ‘This has contributed to the start-up of the project following the assessment of the audit.’ Nor does the Riksantikvaren seem to have made formal demands in connection with the Prince's Gt. 26. Even though the Riksantikvaren gave advice, there is something different than what we thought has been produced as a claim. In the case of the sub-projects, the tunnel and the postal reception, the situation is the same. Nor has there been any requirement from the Riksantik product for the special design of these sub-projects, as far as we can see. The Christian People's Party therefore agrees that what is produced as the demands of the Riksantikvaren cannot be used as a justification for the decisions taken by the Presidency. The National Audit Office has also raised concerns that the plenary session in 2014 did not provide information that the costs of the project would increase, although the increase in costs was known for administrative management prior to the deleveraging process. The information obligation between the Storting and the Government is governed by the Constitution § 82, but there is no similar rule for the relationship between magnification and presidency. Nevertheless, it is important and necessary from our point of view that the general principles of good administration and good practice apply to the Storting as an administrative body. This means that all information necessary for handling cases should be provided. It is a prerequisite that matters dealt with by the Storting should be supported in such a way that the Storting can carry out a proper treatment. That Stortinget did not receive information that the costs would increase beyond what had been reported in the draft budget, believes the Christian People's Party was critical. Nor is information that the increase in costs exceeded the threshold for when external quality assurance needs to be collected, the Stortinget was informed about. The Presidency of the Grand Duchy therefore failed to fulfil its obligation to provide information to the Stortinget. Then the Christian People's Party agrees with the Riksrevision that the Control and Constitutional Committee could and should have requested more detailed information on the development at an earlier date. As the Christian People's Party assesses the matter, we stand behind all the assessments that the Committee has made. We do not support any mistrust proposal. We believe that the Riksrevision has also done a thorough job of clarifying the issues in their mandate, and therefore share the Riksrevision's assessments and conclusions. So we hope that this project can give us a lesson we can use in the organisation of later construction projects here on the house, and it looks like there are already processes going on that are going to do well. We believe that in the Christian People's Party is important and necessary. Otherwise, I refer you to the rapporteur's good review.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e3250	The National Audit Office's investigation into the Great Britain's construction project The Prince's Gate 26, new postal and goods offices and entry tunnel, stands out as both thorough and nuanced and well-lit. A range of representatives, including: The case spokesperson, has had the word, so instead of going through it all one more time, I will briefly present what I focus most on of the critical points. Firstly, the National Audit Office points to very large cost increases in the project, which can only be explained in part by the change in character of the project or by errors made by the consultant. The Court of Auditors particularly points to the increase in costs over the period 2015-2017, where no significant extensions or changes have been made to the project. According to the report, the Administration of the Grand Duchy has presented cost-reducing measures not adopted by the Presidency. Secondly, there was a breach of important guidelines. The project has not been through external quality assurance of cost estimates and governance arrangements, as required by national financial management decisions. According to the National Audit Office, such quality assurance could reveal that the basic material of the project was immature. The size is not subject to the Ministry of Finance's guidance in this area, but could still have followed them when one saw that the cost frame of the project would increase beyond 750 million. kr. In addition, the Riksrevision shows that the purchase regulation was broken in connection with the extension of the design agreement. Thirdly, there was a lack of information for Stortinget, in my opinion. The National Audit Office pointed out that Stortinget was not informed that the expected cost framework would increase when a decision was taken on the project in the State budget for 2014. The Rules of Procedure and the requirements of good conduct require that all information necessary to clarify a matter be presented to the Stortinget. The more complex a matter is, the more important is good information. The National Audit Office points out that the information related to the increase in costs in the 2015 and 2016 budgets was very poor, and this has led to the granting of the guarantee on an incomplete basis. Fourthly, poor distribution and lack of competence have weakened the project and provided the basis for errors. The National Audit Office found that a number of road choices did not provide sufficient analysis. At the same time, according to the National Audit Office, it is clear that the Stortinget did not have enough competence and a strong enough project organisation, especially in the early stages. In the opinion of the National Audit Office, Statsbyggen should also have been included more strongly in the project, and questions are asked about the project model itself. The most important follow-up is a series of proposals on which the Committee is in agreement and which have previously been referred from the Chair. They will make choices, make decisions, acquire skills and ask critical questions. That's it. A similar settled relationship between the Parliament and the Presidency does not exist. However, the Presidency must have – and fortnightly – the confidence of the Grand Duchy if the relations with the plenary are to function. In particular, it is up to the President of the Grand Duchy, with its most central role. The essence of the Riksrevision report is that the project has not been well planned and implemented, that it has not followed all the decisions and instructions that should have been followed, that the Storting in plenary has not been properly informed, and that large cost increases do not have satisfactory explanations.
