ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e3285	The report on the construction case, which is being discussed here today, is a very serious and difficult matter for the Stortinget. Based on the need to rehabilitate the Prince’s Gate 26 and to implement measures to improve the safety of the Stortinget, the first part of the project was initiated in 2011 and extended to include new postal and goods receptions and new downhills to the garage in 2013 and 2014 respectively. The autumn of 2014 started the construction which is still in progress. A good purpose and a solution that delivered several important considerations have ended up in a project in which Stortinget is strongly criticised both by the National Audit Office and by its own ranks for the management of the project at different stages. The case has, of course, also had strong reactions in the general public. Let me first say that this is a criticism I and the rest of the presidency of course take very seriously. The previous and the current presidency must bear a primary responsibility for the serious errors and weaknesses of the construction project, as expressed both in the report of the National Audit Office and in the recommendation of the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs. I would like to stress that this presidency has taken responsibility for the status and development of the project. The Presidency fully acknowledges that this is a very serious matter for the Storting, as the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee points out in its position. There is every reason to complain that the construction case has evolved as it has done, to the detriment of the entire reputation of Storting. In its opinion, the Committee points out that the Riksrevision has selected four areas which it finds problematic. In particular, I will address these in the following and explain how we will address this in the future. The total rehabilitation of the Prince's Gate 26 with its postal and goods reception facilities and entry tunnel is a demanding and very complex project. In retrospect, there is no doubt that the Storting initially underestimated the project significantly. Future construction projects in Stortinget will have to be carried out with, inter alia: significantly higher requirements for competence, capacity, planning and quality assurance. After the rapid rise of problems in 2015, relevant entities in the Administration of the Grand Duchy have also been significantly strengthened in these areas. The size has been reinforced in building engineering and in project management and legal competence, and external quality assurance has been implemented. It's a priority task for the presidency to make sure that we learn from the experience that this project has brought with it. The Presidency has decided to set up a working group to present proposals by the end of the year on the necessary changes to legislation and practices in the planning and implementation of the Stortinge building projects. The Committee supports proposals for a similar evaluation after the construction project, and I agree with that, but therefore believes that there is a basis for starting parts of this work immediately. As the Committee also emphasises in its position, there is a need for a clearer formalisation in administrative matters of roles and responsibilities between the various bodies of the Grand Duchy, i.e. clarification of the roles of the plenary council, the Presidency of the Grand Duchy, budgetary committees and party groups, respectively. It is natural to focus in particular on construction matters and the decision-making process in this context, including the budgetary procedure in such cases. When major construction projects are to be carried out, it must be carried out in such a way that the whole of the Storing understands and participates in the key decisions in the case. The task of the Presidency is to present a draft budget to Stortinget. It is done on behalf of the entire Biggest, but it is up to the Presidency to carry out the necessary investigations and to ensure that the proposals are implemented when they are presented in the State budget. During this phase, it is necessary to provide clearer guidance on how such proposals are anchored in the Storting. More generally, a greater degree of formalisation of the administrative powers of the Presidency and an evaluation of the procedures between the Presidency and the administration in administrative matters should also be carried out. Secondly, legislation to ensure the proper and effective implementation of construction projects in the State shall be fully applicable also to the Grand Duchy. The working group shall consider any necessary adjustments due to the fact that the size is not part of the management but without the result that the rules on the quality assurance of construction projects are less demanding for the Size than for the State. I take note that the Riksrevision believes that Stortinget has not complied with the procurement regulations on a particular point in connection with an extension of an agreement. It was before this presidency's time, but I'm aware that an external assessment of this was made at that time that concluded differently. I note afterwards that the audit came to a different result. It's serious and of course we're going to make sure that not happen again. Thirdly, it is desirable that state-building can serve as support for the Storting as a builder of future projects. The Working Party shall clarify the institutional aspects of such a State Building function and assess how this can be put into practice, of course in dialogue with State Building and the State Department. The Presidency has clearly stated that the objective of this work must be to build state buildings in the future as builders in our contexts. In addition to these three points, central aspects of the project are being worked on in the form of evaluation, both in order to prepare the complaint notified by the Stortinget and to take experience into account for future projects. Experience from the ongoing evaluation carried out by the construction project will form an integral part of the work of the working group. In addition to that already mentioned, the Working Party has been mandated to propose amendments also in other areas if it considers that the experience gained from the construction project and the National Audit Office so warrant. It is my opinion that this report provides a sense of value for the Storting not only in terms of construction, but also more generally in terms of the forms of work we have established. I note that the Supervisory Committee and the Constitutional Committee believe that not enough information was provided to Stortinget in plenary on the running down to the garage in the context of the 2014 budgetary procedure. This question challenges the routines of Stortinget for a long time to investigate administrative matters before a proposal is presented by the Presidency. This concerns what I see as perhaps the most central aspect of this matter – the need for changes in this type of work, so that matters such as the one we are now facing, and administrative measures are significantly better anchored in the plenary and the other bodies of the Storting. Of course, it is a priority for the presidency to ensure that the construction project can be completed according to current plans. Both the Presidency, the Administration and the Project Organisation have worked systematically to achieve this objective within the time and cost frameworks in force. There is now good progress on the construction site, and we are following this work closely with an enhanced organisation. The red thread I think goes through the Riksrevisions and the criticisms of the Control and Constitutional Committee, is, as I see it, about a strong need to make changes to what has built up as a long practice in the Storting in these kinds of cases. The task now is to address this, while the construction project must be completed in a good way. The rapporteur of the case and the chairman of the committee concluded that it was imperative that the presidency should take this and the points proposed. I think I can assure the chairman of the committee, Mr Kolberg, that the presidency is so absolute, and we will do our utmost to start the work that has already been requested, so that this can be continued by a new presidency, which will take place after the elections.

You can go and leave to others to deal with the difficulties, or you can be, and do what you can to correct weaknesses that have been revealed in the light of the experience that has been reaped. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e3455	The Progress Party will point out that in the review of the National Audit Office’s study on the Prince’s Gate 26 construction project, the new postal and goods reception and entry tunnel, there has been a significant lack of project management, a breach of the procurement rules and excessive budget exceedances. The Progress Party supports the Riksrevision’s main conclusion, such as: points out the need to clarify roles and responsibilities between the bodies in the Grand Duchy in administrative matters. Many point to an unfortunate practice, curfew, which has evolved over several periods in the performance of the administrative tasks of the Presidency which are not the equivalent of those of the Grand Duchy's Rules of Procedure. In this construction project, the Stortinget is formally the builder, and it is the administrative management of the Stortinget that manages the building mass of the Stortinget and has responsibility for the project management. The main task of the Grand Duchy's presidency under Section 7 of the Rules of Procedure is to plan and direct the parliamentary work of the Grand Duchy, to ensure that the constitutional rules are complied with, and to represent the Grand Duchy in official contexts. But the Presidentship of the Storting also governs the activities of the Storting administration by, for example, to decide on different actions and priorities and tasks. The presidency of the Grand Duchy is thus part of the administrative management of the Grand Duchy as it is practiced today. The majority has stressed on several occasions the role of the presidency as an administrative body, but without the right to make political decisions. There is a lack of formal and clear guidelines on how to divide roles and responsibilities between presidency and the administration of the Storting. It is therefore possible to ask questions as to whether the Presidency has the necessary formal decision-making powers in addition to those conferred by the Stortinget by the provisions of the Rules of Procedure or by special decisions on a case-by-case basis. The Court of Auditors report on page 106, first paragraph, last paragraph, points out that the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee must take its part in the responsibility for the failure to comply with cost developments or projects at an earlier date. This has been the subject of the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs. However, the size of the plenary as a builder also has an independent responsibility for not having requested more detailed information on the development, so that decisions could be made more fairly. The Board of Directors shall be responsible for the lack of project management, breaches of the procurement rules and large budget excesses, and the Presidentship of the Stortinge shall be responsible for the administrative management of the Stortinge. This case first of all reveals a practice that must cease immediately, and it is good to hear that the president has expressed that they are doing this work well.

The proposal to set up an investigation commission has received broad support. At its hearing on 15 May 29 organisations and individuals participated, all except the Norwegian-Finnish Federation supported the proposal. The Committee has also received written submissions supporting the establishment of the Commission. The assembly plenary also adopted this on 29 May this year, with 37 out of 39 votes. The Municipal and Management Committee has held a hearing and the members of the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party, the Left and the SV join the majority of the Control and Constitution Committee, while the representatives of the Right and the Advance Party join the Right and the Progress Party's views on the Control and Constitution Committee, which oppose the establishment of such a committee. I'm assuming it's being explained by the minority. The starting point of the draft representative is the gathering of indigenous peoples in Norway, their relationship with the Norwegian authorities in the past and the impact history of the policy. However, the proposal also covers women, who have been a national minority in Norway since 1999. In 2005, the government recognised women as a language in Norway. In the research policy, collectives and women/Norwegian finders shared a common destiny. The vast majority of the decisions and instructions from the mid-1900s to the midwar period were common to the parties and women, because they were perceived as border minorities which could pose a threat to the construction of the young Norwegian national state in the border regions. Women have never received a public apology or a complaint from the Norwegian authorities. The majority of the Committee, all except the Right and the Advance Party, believe that the main objective of establishing a Commission must be to establish a common understanding of history. The Commission should also discuss whether the research policy had the same justification for both groups, partners and women, although the content of the policy was the same. It is also important to identify the attitudes taken by research policy to create within the minority society and among the communities and women. This part of the work should also cover today's society. The hearing also revealed a need that many Samaritans and women have to be able to tell their story and be listened to. There is a lot of knowledge about research policy, but much of this knowledge is little known. In the view of the majority, a Commission will change this and contribute to increased knowledge of the majority population. By establishing a Commission, the large community will become more familiar with the research policy. Not least, increased knowledge will make a good contribution to countering similar injustices in the future. The majority of the Committee also considers that a thorough investigation will give authority to further follow-up actions. The majority of the Committee agrees that the Storting should set up a Commission in order to highlight this difficult field. However, we believe that it is necessary to discuss the designation of such a Commission, based on the main objective of providing the Commission with a summary description of the reasons for the search and the concrete actions taken in this respect. The work needs to outline the structure in order to create greater equivalency in relation to the majority culture. The majority indicate that there is a significant economic backlog in e.g.. access to Sami learning materials, lessons in and in Sami at primary school and bets in lule and southern Sami languages. It imposes a responsibility on Stortinget to finance various actions in a satisfactory way, on the basis of Norway’s current commitments. A common understanding of a difficult past will be a very important contribution to reconciliation and justice. The majority believe that the term ‘justice Commission’ can be a more comprehensive name for a Commission, by adding a solid, knowledge-based and unified foundation for our life together. This is primarily a decision of principle. It is proposed that the next presidency should present a proposal for a mandate, composition and name of the Committee for the next enlargement.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e3734	It's nice that it's this historic case that's going to be my last from this pulpit. I have grown up in an environment where Norwegian, Finnish and Sami were part of the everyday life – Sami between Sami, Finnish and Finnish immigrants and Norwegian between us. For us children at home was the Finnish adult language that was used when we were not supposed to understand what they were talking about. As we grew up, there were less and less items of both Sami and Finnish in our everyday lives. We children should not learn Finnish and not at all Sami at school – that's just the way it was. Today many understand that you have missed an incredible amount when you can't speak or understand what my father spoke: Finnish. The same thing applies to Sami. Most of us who were young in the 1960s in Alta cannot speak or understand Sami – a great paradox when we know that there is a language that a large proportion of our inhabitants speak. We should not learn that, but we should learn English. I thank you for that, but today I wish that both Finnish and Sami had been on the agenda for our school days. We could say that we were kind of researched, but to a completely different degree than the Sami and female population. There is no doubt that for several generations both Samer, Women and Norwegian Finns in Norway have been exposed to an active and hard research policy, the aim of which was to assimilate them and other national minorities into Norwegian. It is obvious that research policy had a number of negative consequences for the relationship between the peoples of Norway, not least for the many who knew it on their bodies in their everyday lives, as was evident during the hearing. But it is also a great loss for Norway's language and cultural heritage. We know that there is a lot of knowledge available about research policy. However, it is a challenge that much of this knowledge is known only to a few. It's about how we set up the state reports, because it can also be said with good reason that you have to approximate and find the right balance of the scale, so that it takes care of both the interests, i.e. both the buyer and the seller, in the best possible way.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-20.ana.ud15e4370	The central party believes that public, tax-funded money that goes to child protection should go to child protection services and not to dividends in private, commercial companies. State Child Protection has increased its purchases from private suppliers by between 40 and 50 pst. in the last few years. About 70 pst. of the government purchases in 2015 occurred from commercial companies, and there was a large increase from the previous year. 65 pst. of them they want to reduce this use. In March 2016, the report from Vista Analysis on the use of private actors in child protection in Norway was published. The report’s summary states that private actors play many important roles in child protection. They have to a large extent been able to shape these roles themselves. Government authorities have to a small extent regulated the legal and market conditions, and this places municipal child protection services in a difficult situation, according to the report. These have extensive legal responsibilities, including: for the legal security of children, when they choose to outsource their tasks to private operators. They themselves need to design tools to fulfil these responsibilities, and they themselves need to find out how to manage them in a market strongly influenced by supplier power. The centre party believes that today's practice of short contract periods for child protection challenges the child's right to stable frames around growth. It is an authority's responsibility to ensure a good framework for private non-profit-making measures and institutions, and that contracts are designed in such a way that they also take care of valuable smaller players. Nearer 90 pst. commercial child-resistance services are currently provided by four large groups which have made child-resistance a lucrative industry. The centre party has programmed that child protection services should be run mainly by public and private non-profit organisations and will work for framework conditions that ensure this. We will end the use of competition and supply in the child protection sector. Private non-profit enterprises are an important complement and must secure long-term contracts with the public. From this point of view, we support the SV's proposals today. Public appeals to child protection institutions must comply with the corresponding rules, such as those laid down in § 6-3 of the Free School Act, where it states that the school cannot pay dividends or otherwise transfer profits to the owners or their relatives, and that the school cannot incur costs in terms of rental costs for property or premises belonging to the owners of the school or their relatives, or in any other way incur costs which may imply that the public overburdens or property of the students do not benefit the students. We see no argument that the same principle should not apply to the protection of children, nor is it agreed in the other proposals put forward in the matter.
